    #49 The Browser tier opens an entry on a link-local address

    real use #61real-green

    Repository: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the watch).

    Repository: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the watch).

    The fetch tier refuses an entry whose address is link-local before it connects (src/watch/fetch-source.ts:552, "the entry address is a link-local address and never a source"). The Browser tier asks only refusedAddress (src/watch/browser.ts:123), which judges credentials, the scheme and a malformed address, and src/watch/cdp.ts has no address-kind check. Read in the code, not yet reproduced: a Browser tier entry on http://169.254.169.254/ would be opened by Chrome on the runner.

    Reach: someone whose change to watch/watchlist.json is merged.

    Done when a case fails first — a Browser tier entry on a link-local address reads refused-by-us without Chrome being asked for it — and then passes; the same holds for an IPv6 link-local entry (fe80::/10). The deeper fix, one trust line for both tiers, is the trust-line issue this one blocks.

    Raised by the architecture review of 2026-10-17 (`/improve-codebase-architecture`, scoped by the last 300 commits of both repositories); filed on the human's word to take every candidate. Line numbers are as of site `9f4d6c8` and data `1cb26e8`.

    #12 Four source changes nobody walked

    real-green

    Metric 1 (dataset liveness: open source-change flags older than 48 h, target 0) read 4 on 2026-10-17:

    Metric 1 (dataset liveness: open source-change flags older than 48 h, target 0) read 4 on 2026-10-17:

    • OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data#23 nl-ind-orientation-year, 2026-10-07
    • OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data#24 and #25 fr-f2215-certificat-algerien, 2026-10-08 and 2026-10-09 — a change and its reversal
    • OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data#26 fr-f33952-ict, 2026-10-15 — the FR ICT resources floor, which its own intent line says is SMIC-indexed

    OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data#27 nl-ind-blue-card (2026-10-16) joins it when it turns 48 h.

    The promise broken: the site says re-read daily; a change to a source raises a flag a person walks. The flags were raised; nobody walked them for up to ten days.

    Done when each flag is walked — the changed page read against the quotes it backs, the dataset corrected where a value moved, the flag closed with what was found — and the steward diagnosis says which promise let them sit and what keeps it from happening again.

    Raised by the metric reading on 2026-10-17, when the project moved onto the tracker (`docs/spine/migration-v2.md`).

    Comments

    OytunOnal · 17 Oct 2026

    Walked 2026-10-17. Five flags, OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data#23–#27, each read against every quote its entry backs and closed with what was found. No value moved: four are the text moved and no value did (an unquoted sentence on IND's orientation-year page, a one-day opening-hours line on F2215, page chrome on F33952, two PDF sizes on IND's Blue Card page) and one, #25, is the page came back as it was. The FR ICT resources floor is a fixed euro figure on its page, "1 867,02 € brut par mois", unchanged. Quote fidelity: 195 verified, 0 missing.

    Steward diagnosis.

    • The promise: re-read daily; a change to a source raises a flag a person walks. The first half was kept: every run since 2026-10-01 read all four entries, and each change was flagged the day it happened. The second half was not.
    • Mechanism 1 — an artefact no later step consumes. watch.yml files the flag (gh issue create --label source-change) and stops: no assignee, no mention, no red run. A red run does reach the human: the 2026-10-16 run went red for an unreachable source and filed #27 in the same pass; the red issue was closed the next day, the flag was not.
    • Mechanism 2 — the backstop's clock slipped. The one count of a flag's age is metric 1 (48 h, read every 7 days); it has no writer (#36), so each reading waits on a session remembering it. Read 0 on 2026-09-22, next on 2026-10-17: 25 days.
    • Kept or wrong? Wrong. Followed exactly as written, it would still have let the flags sit: a person walks names no time; the 48 h lives only in metrics.md, held by a weekly reading someone must remember.
    • Precedent: #36 is the same mechanism's second half; not a third time, so no rule is widened.

    The fix is #62, An overdue flag turns the morning red (v1.2.0, WSJF 2.5): the clock in the data repository first, then the site promise says within two days.

    OytunOnal · 17 Oct 2026

    Real use 2026-10-17: metric 1 reads 0 (0 open source-change flags) after the walk; the human: it works. The clock that keeps it is #62.

    #62 An overdue flag turns the morning red

    real use #63mock-green

    Repositories: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the clock), then this one (the promise).

    Repositories: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the clock), then this one (the promise).

    The site promises re-read daily; a change to a source raises a flag a person walks (src/lib/dataset-ld.ts:46; src/lib/copy.ts:295 says it as raises a flag and a person reads it). The promise names no time, so nothing broke it when five flags sat for up to ten days (#12): the watch files a source-change issue and does nothing more — no assignee, no red run — and the one thing that counts a flag's age, metric 1, is read by hand on a 7-day cadence that slipped to 25 days (#36).

    A red run is the one signal shown to reach the human: on 2026-10-16 the same run filed OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data#27 and went red for an unreachable source; the red issue was closed the next day, the flag was not.

    The overdue flag: a flag no person has walked by the second morning after the day it was raised, counted in UTC days — a flag raised on the 16th is overdue on the 18th, whatever hour either run kept (the scheduled 05:17 run has filed its flags between 09:47 and 12:07 UTC, so 48 hours would make the promise two days on some mornings and three on others). It turns the morning's run red on its first overdue morning, as a refusal by us does, because waiting a day changes nothing about a flag nobody is reading. Every open source-change flag counts, a due reminder as much as a detected change.

    Done when, in this order:

    1. Data PR — the clock.
    2. A case fails first and then passes, at two seams: a pure overdueFlags(openFlags, today) (a flag raised on the 16th at 23:59 is overdue on the 18th and not on the 17th; month and year turns) and the exit code of the clock's CLI, which tells overdue apart from the tracker could not be read.
    3. A run with an overdue flag is red and opens one issue, overdue flags (label watch, never source-change, which metric 1 counts), naming each overdue flag with its link, the day it was raised and its age in days; while it stays red, each morning comments on that same issue. The first morning with no overdue flag comments and closes it; a later overdue flag reopens it.
    4. A run whose open flags are all younger is not red for them.
    5. watch run failed is filed only when the check itself did not finish — an unreachable source, a refusal by us, a failed step — and when the tracker cannot be read, which leaves overdue flags untouched.
    6. A measurement run (commit=false) neither reads the clock nor leaves anything behind.
    7. The workflow's issue steps are not unit-tested; the first scheduled morning tests them.
    8. Slice exit on this repository's master. GLOSSARY.md gains Overdue flag below Flag (Avoid: stale flag, unwalked flag, open flag), and Outage says "The run is red — one of the two things a red run means; the other is an Overdue flag."; ARCHITECTURE.md says the watch files an overdue flags issue.
    9. Site PR — the promise, after the clock is merged. The promise is not given before the clock that keeps it:
    10. FRESHNESS_HUMAN_HAND: "A source that has moved raises a flag and a person reads it within two days: …"
    11. the Dataset description: "Re-read daily; a change to a source raises a flag a person reads within two days." (walks is our word, not a reader's.)

    Real use. Mock-green: the tests, and the first scheduled run after the merge logging the clock's reading (0 overdue, the tracker read) and staying green. Real-green: the first real flag after that is either walked within two days with nothing turning red, or turns its second morning red, opens overdue flags, and the morning after its walk closes it.

    Raised by the steward diagnosis of #12 (2026-10-17): the promise was wrong, not broken — followed exactly as written, it would still have let the flags sit. Options weighed with the human: assign the flag (the same unread channel), show waiting flags on `/data/` (honest, but sets no clock), a writer for metric 1 alone (#36; writes the age, wakes nobody). Shaped by `/grill-with-docs` the same day (twelve decisions). WSJF (importance 3 + risk 2) ÷ cost 2 = 2.5.

    #66 The first paint holds still on every arrival

    from #8real use #71mock-green

    Spec of #8, grilled 2026-10-17 (/grill-with-docs #8). It folds in #57 (the first paint owns its covers end to end) and #13 (an unknown link's first paint).

    Spec of #8, grilled 2026-10-17 (/grill-with-docs #8). It folds in #57 (the first paint owns its covers end to end) and #13 (an unknown link's first paint).

    Problem Statement

    A reader who comes back to the interview, or arrives from a country or route page, sees the page move under them before they have done anything.

    • The footer jumps. The build paints question one in the box. Under a cover, the box shows the reader's stand-in line. When the module lands, about 700 ms later on a phone, it draws the reader's own screen, which is not the height of the built one. Everything below moves. Measured on master 0b1b070 with the module held 700 ms:
    • a country link: +249 px at 390x1400;
    • a route link: +246 px;
    • a saved record: +44 px. At 1280x800 the footer is in view, so this is a visible shift on a laptop too.
    • The masthead grows under an unknown link. A link whose code the page does not know (?country=zz) is painted with the short standing promise, the one for a reader who has started. The module then finds nothing answered and writes the long one. The masthead grows 94 px and takes the whole interview with it (CLS 0.0565 at 390x844).
    • The ledger's count widens. It goes from "nothing yet" to the reader's count in view (0.0026).

    The field reads the same thing. Cloudflare metric 6 read 88 % good on 2026-09-23 against a target above 92 %.

    Behind it is a shape that has already made one bug. The first paint's covers are written by the head script, hidden and shown by the page's stylesheet, and taken off in three places in the page's script. One attribute carries two meanings: the cover, and the masthead's standing promise. Taking a cover off is written as "set the promise to started". A record cover left standing once drew a question card with every child invisible (s20's walk). The stylesheet's narrow/wide threshold is a hand copy of the script's.

    Solution

    The first paint holds still for every reader, on every arrival, at every width the product is measured at:

    • A reader who comes back, or arrives from a link, sees the frame and their stand-in line. The footer and the ledger's count take no room, or stay unseen, until the module draws their screen. Then everything appears where it stays.
    • The masthead is always the height of its longer standing promise, so whichever promise the page settles on, nothing below it moves.
    • A fresh visit and a reader owed a verdict see exactly what they see today.

    One module owns the first paint end to end:

    • the head script that writes the covers;
    • the stylesheet rules keyed to them, with the narrow/wide threshold written once;
    • the stand-ins they uncover;
    • a single covers off step the page calls once, in the task that draws the reader's first screen.

    The masthead's standing promise is the page's own state and no longer shares an attribute with a cover.

    User Stories

    1. As a reader returning to an unfinished interview on a phone, I want the page to stay still while my answers come back, so that I am not reading a line that jumps away.
    2. As a reader returning to an unfinished interview on a tall phone (390x1400), I want the footer not to slide down when my question appears, so that the page does not look broken.
    3. As a reader on a laptop (1280x800) returning with a saved record, I want the footer, which I can see, to stay where it is, so that the first thing I see does not lurch.
    4. As a reader who pressed "Check yours" on a country page, I want the page to hold still until my questions are set up, so that I trust what I am looking at.
    5. As a reader who pressed "Check yours" on a route page, I want the same stillness, so that a route arrival is no worse than a country arrival.
    6. As a reader who followed a link with a country or route code the site no longer knows, I want the masthead not to grow under me when the page discovers that, so that a stale link costs me nothing.
    7. As a reader arriving from any link or with a record, I want the stand-in line to be the only thing in the box until my screen is drawn, so that nothing untrue is ever on screen.
    8. As a reader arriving with a record or a link, I want the footer to appear only when my screen is drawn, and where it stays, so that it never moves once seen.
    9. As a reader arriving with a record or a link, I want the ledger's count to appear only when it shows my count, so that I never see "nothing yet" turn into a number.
    10. As a fresh visitor, I want the page to paint once and never change, as it does today, so that the fix for others costs me nothing.
    11. As a fresh visitor, I want the masthead's promise to read as it does today, with at most the space of the longer promise beneath it, so that the page still says what it is.
    12. As a reader who has started, I want the short promise in a masthead that does not resize, so that the page does not jump when I come back.
    13. As a reader owed a verdict, I want the hold state exactly as today (the frame and the hold line, nothing else laid out), so that my return still lands where it stays.
    14. As a reader with JavaScript disabled, I want the page the build painted, unchanged and uncovered, so that nothing is hidden from me by a script that never ran.
    15. As a reader whose browser refuses site data, I want to be treated as a fresh visitor or a link arrival, as today, so that a blocked store never leaves a cover standing.
    16. As a reader who later corrects an answer, starts over, or moves through history, I want no cover from the first paint still standing, so that no card is ever drawn with its children invisible.
    17. As a reader on a narrow screen, I want the ledger folded away and its line above the question from the first paint, as today, so that the ledger never moves after the module lands.
    18. As a reader on a wide screen, I want the ledger to be a panel from the first paint, as today.
    19. As the maintainer, I want every cover the head script can write to have a rule that hides it and a single step that takes it off, provable without a browser, so that s20's bug cannot come back.
    20. As the maintainer, I want the narrow/wide threshold of the first paint's rules to be written once, so that the script and the stylesheet cannot disagree.
    21. As the maintainer, I want the cover and the standing promise to be separate things in the page, so that taking a cover off never rewrites a promise and writing a promise never re-covers anything.
    22. As the maintainer, I want the browser gate to measure every arrival the field sends, including unknown links, at every viewport where the footer can be seen, so that the next shift is caught before it ships.
    23. As the maintainer, I want the browser gate to name the element that moved when a cell fails, so that a failure says where to look.
    24. As the steward, I want the field reading a week after the release to judge the slice, so that a headless zero is not mistaken for the reader's experience.

    Implementation Decisions

    From the grill of #8 (2026-10-17); each was the human's ruling.

    1. One slice, in order. #57's module comes first and is a move: behaviour unchanged, the measurements read as before. #13's and #8's fixes land on it afterwards. Two sequential tickets. They touch the same files, so they are not built in parallel.
    2. Bump Z. It is a fix plus internal work, with no major label and no ADR. The slice stays on v1.2.0.
    3. The first-paint module is deep. Its interface is:
    4. the head script, inlined and hashed into the policy as today;
    5. the stylesheet text for every rule keyed to a cover, a seed or the threshold, which the page inlines in <head>. The policy's style-src already allows inline styles, so the policy does not change;
    6. the threshold, which the page's script still reads for the ledger's live behaviour;
    7. one covers off operation the page calls once, in the task that draws the reader's first screen. The stand-ins stay drawn by the renderer that draws the card; the module names which stand-in each cover uncovers. The module declares its covers as a set: each cover the head script can write, with what it hides (the card's children, the footer, the ledger's count, the masthead's parts) and which stand-in it uncovers. The stylesheet's cover rules are rendered from that set and from nothing else, so the pairing is the contract itself (human's ruling on the standards read of the spec, 2026-10-17).
    8. The cover and the standing promise are separate attributes.
    9. The cover (record, link, verdict) is written only by the head script and removed only by covers off.
    10. The masthead's standing promise (fresh, started) is the page's own state. The page writes it on every screen; the head script seeds it for the first paint.
    11. The ledger's two seeds, answered and narrow, follow the same rule: the head script seeds them for the first paint, and the page's own decisions write them after. Answered still comes down only on a gesture (Start over), never on the arrival.
    12. The footer is covered on record and link arrivals too. Until covers off, it takes no room, as it already does for a reader owed a verdict. Inserted at the page's end, it moves nothing above it.
    13. The ledger's count is covered on record and link arrivals. It stays unseen until covers off, which runs in the task that writes the real count.
    14. Both standing promises share one cell of the masthead. One is visible and the other is hidden in place, so the masthead is always the height of the longer one. This closes #13 without the head script knowing any country or route code. The inline-script rule, that no dataset name is inlined, stands.
    15. The threshold is written once for the first paint's rules. The rule that puts the ledger line above the question on a narrow screen once something is answered moves into the module's stylesheet with the others. The site's own layout breakpoint is out of scope (see below).
    16. Two stray comments by the flag rules are tidied in this slice: one describes the narrow rule apart from where it is, and one is said twice.
    17. Glossary. Cover / stand-in was added on 2026-10-17. A cover is not a flag, which is the watch's word, and taking a cover off is not "started".

    Testing Decisions

    A good test here asserts what the reader sees, or what the module promises at its interface. It never asserts how the page's script is laid out, and it never greps a source file. Two seams, both existing, chosen by the human on 2026-10-17:

    1. The first-paint module's interface, in Node. The existing case that runs the head script in a bottle (a fake document, location, localStorage and matchMedia) is extended. For every reader the head script can recognise:
    2. each cover it writes is a member of the module's declared cover set, and each member hides something;
    3. covers off, run on the bottle's root, leaves no member of the set standing;
    4. covers off leaves the standing promise and the seeds as the page wrote them. The case never reads the stylesheet's text. A substring check on generated CSS would be a content check (CODING_STANDARDS.md, Tests). The set is the contract the stylesheet is rendered from, and whether a rule really hides is the browser gate's to measure. It catches a cover never taken off (s20's bug), a cover outside the set, and a promise wiped with a cover. It cannot see a paint.
    5. The built page in headless Chrome. The existing gate serves dist/ and holds the module back 700 ms. It grows to:
    6. 7 arrivals: cold; saved unfinished record; finished record; ?country=fr; ?route=de-blue-card-general; ?country=zz; ?route=zz.
    7. 4 viewports: 390x844, 390x1400, 1280x900, and 1280x800 added. 1280x900 stays because the case ids carry the viewport's name. Existing ids keep their names; new cells get new ids.
    8. In every cell:
    9. CLS is exactly 0, not under the "good" bound of today;
    10. nothing above the box moves;
    11. on record and link arrivals the footer has no box until the module has drawn;
    12. a failure names the node that moved.
    13. The existing cases stay: the stand-in says something true, the resumed line, the screen the row lands on, the same box to the pixel, the cold page keeps the built node, and the ledger panel or fold.

    Prior art: tests/first-paint.test.ts holds both seams today, the bottle and the browser gate, and the bottle is its Node half. The suite split rule does not apply: nothing moves between files.

    Mock-green: the 7 × 4 table reads CLS 0 in every cell, with the module held 700 ms, on the branch's build.

    Real-green: a week after the release, Cloudflare Web Analytics with the same filter as the 2026-09-15 reading (bots excluded, last 7 days). Metric 6 must read CLS good above 92 %, and no debug row may name #app, footer.site-foot or the masthead. LCP and INP must be no worse than the 2026-09-15 reading (LCP good 91 %, INP good 100 %).

    Out of Scope

    • `html>body>div.wrap` CLS 0.181, 2 pageviews of / in the 2026-09-23 reading. It was not reproduced on any arrival or viewport on 2026-10-17. Nothing on master was found to move div.wrap: there are no web fonts and no images, and scrollbar-gutter: stable holds the column. Two causes were not measured: Android zooming out on a screen that overflows sideways, and visits before s31 shipped. If the real-green reading names div.wrap again, it becomes its own issue with no milestone, starting with that measurement.
    • The site's narrow/wide breakpoint, written by hand in the shared stylesheet, the route, data and feedback pages, and the interview's layout blocks. It is its own issue with no milestone, proposed on 2026-10-17.
    • Shifts after the reader's first input. They are input-excluded, and are not this slice's.
    • The first paint of any page but the interview.

    Further Notes

    Measurements on master 0b1b070 (2026-10-17, module held 700 ms / not held), from a headless run of the site's own browser helpers:

    arrival390x844390x14001280x800
    fresh /0 / 00 / 00 / 0
    saved unfinished.0026 / 0.0301 / 0.0008 / .0008
    saved finished0 / 00 / 00 / 0
    ?country=fr.0026 / 0.055 / 0.0048 / .0048
    ?route=de-blue-card-general.0026 / 0.0543 / 0.0034 / .0034
    ?country=zz.0565 / 0.0454 / 0.016 / .016
    ?route=zz.0565 / 0.0454 / 0.016 / .016

    At 1280 Chrome painted about 100 ms before the module even unheld, so those rows shift with no delay at all.

    #72 A flag's excerpt is the page's; what a run says leaves the CLI

    from #14real use #75mock-green

    Spec for #14, "A flag quotes a page; the quote is the page's". It folds in #54, "What a watch run says, out of the CLI". Repository: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the watch). Bump: Z.

    Spec for #14, "A flag quotes a page; the quote is the page's". It folds in #54, "What a watch run says, out of the CLI". Repository: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the watch). Bump: Z.

    Problem Statement

    A curator walks a flag by reading the issue the watch opened. In that issue, the page's words around the change are printed as a Markdown blockquote. Markdown still acts inside a blockquote, so the page can act on our tracker:

    • an @login in a source's text notifies a stranger;
    • a #12 leaves a cross-reference on one of our issues;
    • a [text](url) becomes a link, and emphasis restyles what the curator reads;
    • an &lt;img src=…&gt; in the page survives tag stripping, because entities are decoded after tags are removed, and renders as an image.

    Two kinds of character also pass the text normalizer: bidi overrides and isolates, and control bytes such as ESC. A bidi override reorders what follows it in the issue. An ESC starts an escape sequence in the terminal of a curator who prints the flag file.

    Our own words run into the page's. The ellipsis marks around the excerpt are ours, but they sit inside the page's text. The flag's sections are joined without blank lines, so the remediation sentence renders as part of the page's quotation: watch/flags/nl-ind-blue-card-2026-10-16.md shows it.

    The excerpt is cut by UTF-16 units. A cut can fall between the two halves of an astral character, and the flag then shows a replacement character the page never wrote.

    Sanitising the page's words would change what the curator reads. This is why the fix is the flag's format, not its content.

    The code that writes a flag cannot be tested, which is how all of this went unseen. The watch's CLI does its decision work at module top level and ends in process.exit. That work is the flag's body and name, the remediation, the log level per outcome, the --only filter, the read_at line and the BOM-tolerant read. The tests can only check flags already committed, not the code that writes them (#54).

    Solution

    A flag's excerpt is set apart so that nothing in it acts on the page it is shown in, and every word around it is ours:

    • The excerpt sits in a fenced code block that its own text cannot close.
    • Characters that steer how text is drawn are dropped, and control bytes become a space. This is the rule the watch already applies to every page-written fragment it prints.
    • No other character of the page's words is changed.
    • Our sentence introducing the excerpt stands outside the block, and blank lines separate each section of the flag.
    • The excerpt is cut on characters, never inside one.

    What a finished run says moves into a module of its own: its flags, its log events and its exit code, returned as data. The CLI only reads files, writes them and exits. The flag's format is then asserted by tests that call that module. One of those tests renders the flag with a real Markdown renderer.

    User Stories

    1. As a curator, I want the page's words in a flag to be shown exactly as the page wrote them, so that I judge the change the source made and not a rewritten version of it.
    2. As a curator, I want an @login written by a source to stay plain text in the issue, so that no stranger is notified by our tracker.
    3. As a curator, I want a #12 written by a source to stay plain text, so that our issues do not collect cross-references a page put there.
    4. As a curator, I want links, images and emphasis written by a source to show as the characters they are, so that nothing a page wrote becomes something I can click or something that loads.
    5. As a curator, I want a run of backticks in a source's text to be unable to close the block around it, so that a page cannot end the excerpt and write into the rest of the issue.
    6. As a curator, I want bidi overrides and isolates dropped from the excerpt, so that the issue shows the words in the order the page stored them.
    7. As a curator who prints a flag file in a terminal, I want control bytes such as ESC shown as a space, so that a page cannot steer my terminal.
    8. As a curator, I want the sentence that introduces the excerpt to sit outside the excerpt and to say it is cut at both ends, so that I know which words are the page's and that the cut is ours.
    9. As a curator, I want the remediation to be its own paragraph, so that I never read our advice as something the page said.
    10. As a curator, I want no replacement character at either end of an excerpt, so that every character I read is one the page wrote.
    11. As a curator, I want a flag whose excerpt prints nothing to say so and to send me to the page, so that I do not stare at an empty block.
    12. As a curator, I want a flag's header (title, url, kind, date, hashes) to stay one block, so that a resolution I append below it stays below the record the run wrote.
    13. As a curator, I want flags written before this change left as they were written, so that each flag still says what its run saw.
    14. As a curator, I want a sentinel's flag to keep its own remediation, and every other flag to take its strategy's, so that the advice still matches the kind of entry.
    15. As a curator, I want reminder flags to keep their stable, self-overwriting name and change flags their dated name, so that reminders do not pile up and history stays visible.
    16. As the maintainer, I want the flag's format asserted by a test that calls the code which writes it, so that a format change cannot slip through by passing tests that read old files.
    17. As the maintainer, I want a test to render a hostile flag with a real Markdown renderer, so that "the block cannot be closed" is the renderer's answer and not our reading of the spec.
    18. As the maintainer, I want the log level for each outcome, the unread-source notices and the closing watch complete line decided in a module, so that what a morning's log says is testable.
    19. As the maintainer, I want the exit code decided from the verdict in a module, so that the one signal the workflow reads is asserted by a test.
    20. As the maintainer, I want --only with an unknown id to be a decision a test can call, so that the "no such watch entry" answer and its exit code are pinned.
    21. As the maintainer, I want the read_at decision (log only when the reading was taken at another address) in the module, so that both tiers follow one rule that a test reads.
    22. As the maintainer, I want the BOM-tolerant parse of a hand-edited watchlist or state to be a function of text, so that a Windows editor's BOM is a tested case rather than a comment.
    23. As the maintainer, I want the CLI to hold only I/O, so that every branch that decides something is in reach of a test.
    24. As the maintainer, I want the move into the module to change no byte of what a run writes and logs, so that the format change in the second ticket is the only behaviour change and reads alone in its diff.
    25. As the workflow (watch.yml), I want the exit code, the flag file names and the first line of each flag to keep their current meaning, so that collecting new flags and opening their issues keep working untouched.
    26. As a future writer of flags as GitHub issues, I want the flag documents returned as data, so that I can reuse the format without the file system.
    27. As a reader of the glossary, I want the flag's excerpt named apart from a dataset quote, so that "quote" keeps meaning a sentence the dataset stands on.

    Implementation Decisions

    Excerpt is the glossary's word (#14, now in GLOSSARY.md): the page's own words around the first difference, as the run read them. It is cut at both ends, carries nothing that steers how text is drawn, and is otherwise never rewritten. A quote stays the dataset's. The report's field for it is renamed from context to excerpt. No log line or committed file carries that name today.

    The excerpt's block.

    • The excerpt is written in a fenced code block with the info string text.
    • The fence is made of backticks, one longer than the longest run of backticks in the excerpt, and never shorter than three. The page's text cannot close it.
    • The excerpt is one line, because the normalizer has already collapsed the page's whitespace.

    What is taken out of the excerpt.

    • It passes the watch's existing printable rule, which lives in the failure module: every Default_Ignorable_Code_Point is dropped, and runs of whitespace and control bytes (C0, DEL, C1) collapse to one space.
    • No length bound is added, because the cut already bounds it.
    • The rule stays in one place. The flag asks for it; nobody writes a second copy.
    • Nothing else changes: words, punctuation and Markdown characters are left as the page wrote them.

    What stands outside the block.

    • The ellipsis marks leave the excerpt.
    • A line of ours stands directly above the fence: "The page now reads, around the first difference (cut at both ends):".

    An empty excerpt.

    • If the excerpt prints nothing once the rule has run, there is no introducing line and no fence.
    • One line of ours stands in their place: "The page's words around the first difference print nothing; read the page."
    • A report with no excerpt (a reminder, a pdf bytes change) writes neither, as today.

    The flag's sections.

    • The title line and the field lines (url, kind, date, old, new) are one block with no blank line inside it. The committed-flags test reads the header as everything up to the first blank line.
    • After that block, every section is separated by one blank line: the intent, the excerpt (or its empty line), and the remediation.
    • Field values stay as today: the url through the printable address rule, and the intent as the watchlist's own words.

    The cut.

    • The excerpt is cut by Unicode code points, not UTF-16 units. The first difference and both ends of the span are found in characters.
    • The span stays as it is: 60 characters before the first difference and 120 from it.

    The module, "what a run says". A new watch module turns a finished run into what it says, as data:

    • The run. Input: the reports, the verdict, today, and what became of the state, as data rather than a flag: either "committed", with the entry count, the targeted id (if any) and last_run, or "dry run". Output:
    • the flag documents, each a name and a body. A reminder takes <id>-reminder.md and a change <id>-<date>.md. Only changed and reminder-due produce one.
    • the log events, each a level, a message and its fields: one per report, at its level per outcome; the unread-source notices; the state committed or dry-run line; and watch complete with the run summary.
    • the exit code: 1 when the verdict is red, 0 otherwise.
    • The remediation moves with the flag: a sentinel's own sentence, except on a due reminder, and otherwise the strategy's sentence from the strategy table.
    • The `--only` choice. Given the watchlist and the argument, it returns the entries to run, or "no such watch entry" with exit code 2.
    • The `read_at` decision. Given the entry and a fetch result, it returns a read_at event when an ok reading was taken at another address, and nothing otherwise.
    • The BOM-tolerant parse takes text and returns the parsed object. Reading the file stays in the CLI.
    • What stays in the CLI: reading the watchlist and state files, opening and closing Chrome and logging each browser read's cost, running the watch, writing flag files and the state file, printing log lines, and exiting.
    • Ticket 1 changes nothing. Moving the decisions changes no byte of any flag, log message or exit code.

    The order. Two tickets, the second blocked by the first:

    1. What a run says, out of the CLI (#54). This ticket changes no behaviour.
    2. The flag's excerpt is the page's (#14). It brings the block, the printable rule, the line of ours, the sections, the empty excerpt and the code-point cut.

    A new dev dependency. A CommonMark parser with its GFM extension (micromark and micromark-extension-gfm) joins devDependencies for the render test. It goes into the lockfile and the audit, and never into the runtime or the site's build.

    Testing Decisions

    A good test here asserts what a curator would see and what the workflow reads, through the module's interface. It never asserts the module's internals, and it never greps a source file. Expected values come from literals written by hand, never from the code's own rule recomputed.

    Seam 1: the new module (agreed 2026-10-18). Its prior art is #62's overdue module and its CLI, tested in tests/overdue.test.ts.

    • Format. A report whose excerpt carries @login, #12, [x](y), <img src=x>, a run of three backticks, a right-to-left override, an ESC and an emoji:
    • It is rendered with the GFM parser. The page's words appear only inside one <pre><code>. No <a>, <img> or mention comes from them. The introducing line and the remediation are outside the code block.
    • The text inside the fence equals a hand-written literal: the override gone, the ESC a space, everything else as the page wrote it.
    • The fence is longer than the excerpt's longest backtick run, and a run of four backticks in the excerpt still cannot close it.
    • Empty excerpt. An excerpt of steering characters only gives no fence and the "print nothing" line.
    • Header. The header block, up to the first blank line, carries the url, the date and the hashes, as the committed-flags test expects.
    • Remediation. A sentinel change takes the sentinel sentence, a sentinel reminder takes the strategy's, and a pdf-text change takes its own.
    • Names. A reminder gets the stable name and a change the dated one. unchanged, ok and unreachable produce no flag.
    • Log and exit.
    • Each outcome maps to its level: unreachable is error; unchanged and ok are info; the rest are warn.
    • A red verdict exits 1 and a green one 0. The watch complete event carries the run summary.
    • A "committed" outcome gives the state committed event with its count, targeted id and last_run. A "dry run" gives the dry-run line.
    • `--only`. An unknown id gives "no such watch entry" and exit 2. A known one gives that entry alone.
    • `read_at`. A reading taken elsewhere gives one event. A reading at its own address, or a failed one, gives none.
    • Parse. Text with a leading BOM parses the same as without it.
    • Ticket 1's proof. Its cases are written against today's format, so they pass before and after the move. Ticket 2 then changes only the format cases.

    Seam 2: `runWatch` (existing; called 28 times with a fake fetcher in the watch tests).

    • A page whose first difference sits next to an astral character, with the cut falling inside it under UTF-16, gives an excerpt with no lone surrogate at either end.
    • The excerpt starts and ends where a character-counted span says it does.

    The existing case "a resolved flag still says what it saw" stays as it is and keeps passing over the 22 committed flags.

    Out of Scope

    • Rewriting the 22 committed flags, or the issues already opened from them. They stay as their runs wrote them.
    • A writer that opens flags as GitHub issues directly. The documents are returned as data so one can be written later.
    • The title line and the intent. Both are our words (the entry's id and outcome, and the watchlist's note), and neither is changed.
    • Error text in log lines. Errors are printed by the failure module's bounds and never reach a flag.
    • The workflow watch.yml. Its file collection and issue creation are unchanged.

    Further Notes

    • Grilled 2026-10-18 (Q1–Q12). The fenced block over escaping, the printable rule, our words outside, the code-point cut, the module's boundary, the committed flags, the render test, Excerpt, Z, real use, two tickets and the empty excerpt were each ruled by the human in turn.
    • Mock-green.
    • The tests above pass on the build.
    • I also take a real page's text from watch/state.json, put hostile fragments into it, produce a flag through the module, and render it through GitHub's own renderer (gh api markdown, gfm). The excerpt must come back in one code block, with no mention, link or image, and our lines outside.
    • Real-green (a later event). The first source-change issue the daily watch opens after the merge. The human opens it on GitHub and checks three things:
    • the page's words are inside a code block;
    • the "The page now reads…" line and the remediation are outside it;
    • the words match the changed page.

    The retro waits for that issue.

    Tickets

    • #73 What a run says, out of the CLI
    • #74 A flag's excerpt is the page's

    Comments

    OytunOnal · 18 Oct 2026

    Ruling from the review of OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data#30 (2026-10-18), recorded here instead of editing the body:

    • The line above the fence now reads "The page now reads, around the first difference (cut where the page goes on):", not "(cut at both ends)". An excerpt whose first difference is near the page's start or end is not cut at that end, and the line must be true for every excerpt.
    • No blank line separates that line from the fence. The blank lines between the sections stay.

    Fixed in the fix round, at e65cdfd on excerpt-is-the-pages. The glossary's Excerpt follows with the next master commit.

    #76 A route page's headline dates its value by a source the page never shows

    real use #79mock-green

    Blocker B2 of the v1.2 gate critique (docs/spine/critiques/2026-10-18-v1.2-gate-critique.md).

    Blocker B2 of the v1.2 gate critique (docs/spine/critiques/2026-10-18-v1.2-gate-critique.md).

    What a reader sees

    The top of /germany/experienced-worker/ reads:

    €45,630/year … Read from the authority's own page on 2026-09-17, with the sentence it came from below

    The sentence below it says "read 2026-09-02".

    Why

    It is one fact taken from the wrong source, not two facts.

    • The headline: the panel (answerBlock, src/lib/route-page.ts) prints the page's stamp. The stamp is the newest date among the route's values and every source of the audience notice: source, plus sources. The 2026-09-17 comes from notices[eu-free-movement].sources: the IND EEA sentence and the Swiss sentence. The route page never prints those; its rule card shows only notice.source, dated 2026-09-03.
    • The quote: it prints the threshold's own retrieved_at, 2026-09-02. Its history is empty, so no "confirmed unchanged" date exists for the headline to mean.
    • The glossary: Provenance calls both a retrieval date. A "read" date comes only from a value's own reading or its reading history.

    How far it goes

    • The date: all 23 scored route pages show 2026-09-17. None of the 185 values on route pages carries that date.
    • The figure: 16 panels name a figure read between 2026-09-02 and 2026-09-08.
    • No figure: 7 panels say "No salary or points threshold", and still promise "the sentence it came from below".
    • Elsewhere: the country cards and the masthead use the same stamp.

    Done when

    • Each headline value is dated by its own retrieved_at, the date its quote prints.
    • A page's stamp is drawn only from sources the page prints.
    • A panel with no figure does not promise a sentence below it.
    • A case pins each of these as a decision, on a route whose notice carries more sources than it prints.

    No new data; no second, "confirmed unchanged" date. That would need a decision on which watch fact backs it.

    No new term for GLOSSARY.md; no ADR (every decision is a two-way door).

    #104 The trust line: one module decides where the watch may go

    from #50real use #109real-green

    Repository: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the watch). Specced from #50 and its grill decisions (#50, comment of 2026-10-19).

    Repository: OytunOnal/permit-rulebook-data (the watch). Specced from #50 and its grill decisions (#50, comment of 2026-10-19).

    Problem Statement

    The watch's trust line (where the watch may go) is written in several places. Most of it lives inside the HTTP adapter, the fetcher. The browser reader borrows one piece of it: the entry check. The browser's landing check spells the same-origin rule a second time. The coverage gate spells the credentials rule a third time. The core and the fetcher import each other. A curator, a maintainer or a reviewer cannot read the rule in one place. A change to it must find every copy, and a copy that is missed is a gap between the two readers; the link-local gap #49 closed was one.

    Two holes found in #49's review are still open:

    • An address inside the NAT64 local-use prefix 64:ff9b:1::/48 reads as public. [64:ff9b:1::a9fe:a9fe], which carries the address a hosted runner keeps its credentials behind, passes, while the same address under 64:ff9b::/96 is refused.
    • The coverage gate lets a link-local entry into the watchlist. Both readers refuse it on the run, so a curator learns of it the morning after the merge, as a red run, instead of at the PR.

    Solution

    One deep module owns the trust line. It answers four questions, one for each moment a source has:

    • the entry address;
    • a redirect target;
    • the addresses a name resolves to;
    • the page a reader ended on.

    Each answer is a refusal, which names the kind of address and never the address, or nothing. Beside the four questions sit the printers, which show an address the way a person should see it. Both readers and the coverage gate ask the module; none of them spells any part of the rule. The fetcher keeps only HTTP work.

    As a result:

    • the gate refuses exactly what the run would refuse, so a curator hears of a link-local entry before the merge;
    • every address in the NAT64 local-use prefix is private, so a public source cannot send the watch there;
    • nothing a reader of the site or a curator sees changes otherwise: the same 46 sources are read in the same way.

    User Stories

    1. As a maintainer of the watch, I want the trust line in one module, so that a change to where the watch may go is a change to one file.
    2. As a maintainer, I want both readers to ask the same module, so that an address one reader refuses is refused by the other by construction.
    3. As a maintainer, I want the same-origin rule written once, so that the fetcher's redirect loop and the browser's landing check cannot drift apart.
    4. As a maintainer, I want the fetcher to hold only HTTP work, so that I can read its requests, bodies and budgets without reading address rules among them.
    5. As a maintainer, I want the core and the fetcher to stop importing each other, so that the planned dependency check (#37) has two fewer exceptions to carry.
    6. As a maintainer, I want the module to import nothing but the failure wording, so that it sits beneath every caller and can never close a cycle.
    7. As a maintainer, I want the redirect setting, same-origin or anywhere, to be the trust line's own type, so that the strategy table names a rule the module owns.
    8. As a maintainer, I want the address kinds kept inside the module, so that no caller decides anything from a kind on its own.
    9. As a maintainer, I want the lookup hook to stay in the fetcher and ask the module, so that Node's socket machinery and the rule stay in separate modules.
    10. As a curator, I want the coverage gate to refuse a link-local entry, so that I hear it at the PR and not as a red morning.
    11. As a curator, I want the gate to refuse exactly what the run refuses (malformed, credentialled, not a page, link-local), so that a green gate means the run will open the entry.
    12. As a curator, I want the gate's refusal to name the entry and the kind of address, never the address itself, so that a credential in a url never reaches a log.
    13. As a curator, I want today's 46 entries to pass the stricter gate unchanged, so that the change asks nothing of me.
    14. As the person who runs the watch, I want every address in 64:ff9b:1::/48 treated as private, so that a public source cannot use a local NAT64 gateway to reach the address where a hosted runner keeps its credentials.
    15. As the person who runs the watch, I want the local-use prefix refused whatever the operator's prefix length, so that the rule does not depend on where an operator chose to place the carried IPv4 address.
    16. As the person who runs the watch, I want every spelling of an address judged by the one address it means (IPv4-mapped, the compat form, NAT64 /96, 6to4, site-local, the two names for this machine), exactly as today, so that the move loses nothing.
    17. As the person who runs the watch, I want the relative rule kept (a source may not send the watch somewhere it could not have gone itself), so that a fixture on loopback still reads within loopback.
    18. As the person who runs the watch, I want a refusal at any of the four moments to stay refused-by-us, so that the run's colour is unchanged.
    19. As the person who runs the watch, I want every refusal sentence to read as it does today, so that the log, the flags and the issues say the same things after the move.
    20. As a reader of a failure issue, I want an address printed without its credentials and bounded at 200 characters, cut on characters, exactly as today, so that nothing a page chose can make the issue unreadable.
    21. As the site, I want a report's url stripped of credentials and otherwise unchanged, so that the unread list still keys on the dataset's own source urls.
    22. As a maintainer, I want the rule tested as a pure table, with no HTTP server and no Chrome, so that a new spelling or kind is one row and runs in milliseconds.
    23. As a maintainer, I want one wiring case per caller, so that a caller that stopped asking the module goes red even though the table stays green.
    24. As a reviewer, I want the moved comments to state today's reasons without history, so that the module reads as the rule and not as its diary.
    25. As a reader of the glossary, I want "trust line" to name the whole rule and "floor" retired, so that the code, CONTRIBUTING.md and the glossary use one word.
    26. As a maintainer, I want the browser reader's stated gap (a name Chrome resolves for itself) kept in its own header, so that what the trust line does not read is written down.
    27. As the human, I want real use to be the first scheduled watch after the merge reading as the day before, so that my only step is the word.

    Implementation Decisions

    • The trust line module is new, in the watch. Its interface:
    • The entry question: given an entry address, a refusal when it is malformed, carries a name and password, is on a scheme that is not a page, or is link-local. Otherwise nothing.
    • The redirect question: given a target, the entry's host and the redirect setting, a refusal when the target is not a page, carries credentials, is link-local, breaks the relative rule, or leaves the origin under same-origin. Otherwise nothing. The hop cap stays the fetcher's: it is about loops, not places.
    • The resolution question: given a name, every address it resolved to and the entry's host, a refusal when any one address is refused, naming the kind and never the address. Otherwise nothing.
    • The landing question: given the origin asked for, the address the reader ended on and the redirect setting, a refusal when the reading left the entry's origin under same-origin. Otherwise nothing.
    • The printers: an address without its credentials (for a report); the same, bounded, for a person (for a log, a flag or an issue); a bound for any address-shaped field; and the bound itself, which the browser reader's in-page code also uses.
    • The redirect setting's type, same-origin or anywhere, now owned here. The core's strategy table imports it.
    • Names: the module's exported functions are verbs (CODING_STANDARDS.md, Naming), for example judgeEntry, judgeRedirect, judgeResolution, judgeLanding, stripCredentials, shortenAddress, printAddress. The old names go with the move; none is kept beside the new ones.
    • The address kinds, the spelling-to-meaning parser and the relative rule are internal: none is exported.
    • `64:ff9b:1::/48`: every address under the NAT64 local-use prefix is private, never unwrapped. The operator chooses where the IPv4 address sits inside that prefix (RFC 8215, RFC 6052 lengths), so unwrapping a fixed position would close one placement and miss the others. 64:ff9b::/96 keeps unwrapping its last 32 bits.
    • The module's dependencies: it imports only the failure module, which owns the wording of a shortened field and imports nothing. The core imports the module; the module imports nothing from the core.
    • The fetcher (the HTTP adapter) keeps its request, the hop loop, the hop cap, the budget, the body bounds, the resolver seam, the system resolver and the lookup hook. The hook asks the resolution question and hands the socket exactly the judged list, as today. The redirect loop asks the redirect question. The entry asks the entry question.
    • The browser reader asks the entry question before Chrome is asked, as today. The browser's landing check asks the landing question and no longer spells the origin rule. When the setting is anywhere, it bounds the address it reports through the module's printer, as today.
    • The coverage gate's credentials check becomes the entry question, so the gate refuses malformed, credentialled, non-page and link-local entries. Its line names the entry id and the module's reason; as today, nothing past an origin is printed.
    • The run's report printer prints addresses through the module, not through the fetcher.
    • Refusal sentences, failure classes and printed shapes are unchanged except the gate's new reasons. No output a curator or the site reads changes for today's watchlist.
    • Vocabulary: "trust line" names the whole rule. "Floor" is retired from the code's names and comments and from CONTRIBUTING.md. The site's GLOSSARY.md has the entry (with the site's docs commit).
    • Comments: each moved comment states today's constraint or reason. Dates, review names, slice numbers and "learnt in" narration go (CODING_STANDARDS.md, Rules: "No history").
    • Docs in the data repository: CONTRIBUTING.md's refused-by-us row and its paragraph on resolution say "trust line". Any doc or header that names the fetcher as the owner of the address rule names the module instead.
    • Bump: Z, internal, in v1.2.1.

    Testing Decisions

    • A good test here asks a question at an interface and asserts the decision: refused or not, the failure class, and that a refusal carries no address past what the contract allows. It never asserts a sentence's wording, except the contract's own pair: the kind is named, the address is not.
    • Seam 1, the main one: the trust line's four questions and its printers, as pure tables. The rows cover every spelling the parser reads (IPv4 in each normalised form, IPv6 with ::, an embedded dotted quad, IPv4-mapped, compat ::/96, NAT64 /96, NAT64 local-use /48 new, 6to4, site-local, unique-local, link-local, localhost and its subdomains, :: and ::1), each against a public entry and a loopback entry. They also cover credentials and scheme at the entry and redirect moments, the relative rule, a name with one refused address among allowed ones, and the same-origin rule at the redirect and landing moments under both settings. The printers get credentials stripped, the bound on characters, and astral characters not split.
    • Seam 2, the fetcher, with an injected resolver and a local server: a redirect to a refused target is never requested and reads refused-by-us, and a name that resolves to a private address from a public entry is refused before any request. Prior art: the s34 fetcher suite and s36's resolution cases.
    • Seam 3, the browser reader: a link-local entry is refused before Chrome is asked (prior art: #49's case). The existing real-Chrome off-site cases stay as they are and cover the landing question's wiring.
    • Seam 4, the coverage gate: a link-local entry is refused, and the line names the entry and not the address. Prior art: the gate's existing credentials case.
    • Moved, not duplicated: the cases that judge an address through the fetcher's server (most of s34's floor cases, s36's prefix cases) become rows of seam 1. The cases about HTTP work (headers, encodings, body bounds, budgets, failure classes, the hop cap, read_at) stay where they are.
    • The new NAT64 local-use row and the gate's link-local case each fail before their code.
    • Green: npm run check end to end in the data repository.

    Out of Scope

    • Judging an address Chrome resolves for itself. Chrome takes no lookup hook. Closing that gap is new behaviour and a design of its own (an IP check after the connection, or a resolver pin that covers only the entry host). It stays the accepted gap stated in the browser reader's header; an issue with no milestone, if the human asks for one.
    • A stricter gate than the run (for example, refusing private or loopback entries in the watchlist). The gate asks the entry question and nothing more.
    • Changing any refusal sentence, failure class, the hop cap or a printed bound.
    • The dependency check itself (#37).

    Further Notes

    • Real use: the first scheduled watch on data master after the merge reads as the day before did: the same 46 sources, the same seven browser entries, failing only where they already fail (de-bmi-chancenkarte's outage since 2026-10-15), and no new refused-by-us. The session reads and compares the two runs and posts the reading; the human's step is the word.
    • At the slice's exit, ARCHITECTURE.md's Watch line stops pointing Chrome's own resolution at #50, and names the trust line.
    • All 46 watchlist entries are named hosts (checked 2026-10-19), so the stricter gate refuses none of them.

    Tickets

    • #105 The trust line answers four questions, and the fetcher asks it
    • #106 The browser reader and the run's printers ask the trust line; the old spellings are gone
    • #107 The coverage gate asks the trust line's entry question
    • #108 Every address in the NAT64 local-use prefix is private

    #110 The site's address is reviewed code, and the asset carry is a deep module

    from #46real use #115real-green

    From #46 (folds #59 and #26), grilled on 2026-10-20.

    From #46 (folds #59 and #26), grilled on 2026-10-20.

    Problem Statement

    The site's address comes from a repository variable, a setting that no code review ever reads. Whoever can write this repository's variables decides where the deploy believes the site lives. That decides three things:

    • where the carry reads the live site from: a host they control could serve a page naming a real previous-generation asset, with a digest list attesting hostile bytes under that name, and the deploy would publish those bytes from our own origin, where the Content-Security-Policy's 'self' covers them and no integrity hash checks them;
    • the canonical links and the sitemap every page is built with;
    • the host IndexNow is told about.

    #46's finding came from s33's review, which narrowed it over six rounds but never closed it.

    Beside it, the carry is one file of 1034 lines that holds four levels: the rules, the flow, the network reads and the command line. The flow calls the network directly, so every one of its 59 test cases starts a real HTTP server. The digest list, a term of its own in the glossary, lives inside the carrier with no seam of its own (#26). The same file reads the address and decides what to trust (#59).

    Solution

    The site's address is one literal line in the deploy workflow, https://permitrulebook.com/, reviewed like any code. The workflow reads no repository variable, and a test keeps it that way. The build, the sitemap, IndexNow and the carry all get the address from that line. After the merge, the human deletes the variable.

    The carry becomes a deep module. The deploy's command does not change. Behind it, five private files hold one level each:

    • which references in a live page are assets;
    • the digest list's format;
    • the wording of a stranger's string in our log;
    • the flow;
    • the HTTP reader of the live site.

    The flow is given the live site's reader as an input. Its rules are tested against an in-memory live site, and a real server is kept only for the HTTP reader's own cases.

    Nothing changes for a reader. The same site is published at the same address, and a cached page still finds its assets.

    User Stories

    1. As the maintainer, I want the site's address decided in reviewed code, so that nobody can move it without a commit I can read.
    2. As the maintainer, I want the deploy to read no repository variable at all, so that the settings page holds no lever over what the site publishes.
    3. As the maintainer, I want a test to fail when someone adds a repository variable read to the deploy workflow, so that the decision outlives my memory of it.
    4. As the maintainer, I want changing the domain to stay a one-line edit, so that the test guards the decision and not the value.
    5. As a reader whose browser cached a page, I want the deploy to keep carrying the previous generation's assets, so that the page I have still finds what it asks for.
    6. As a reader, I want every carried byte to come only from our own live site and match the previous build's digest list, so that nothing a stranger served reaches my browser from our origin.
    7. As a reader, I want canonical links and the sitemap to name the real site, so that search engines and shared links lead to it.
    8. As someone who can write this repository's variables, I find no setting that moves the site's address, so the reach #46 named is gone.
    9. As the maintainer, I want the carry's command in the deploy to stay exactly as it is, so that the step's pinned block and its position before the upload do not move.
    10. As the maintainer, I want the carry to still exit 0 on every path, so that it can never fail a deploy.
    11. As the maintainer, I want the carry's last lines to still say which of the three outcomes happened, so that a healthy deploy is still told from a silent one.
    12. As the maintainer, I want the carry to still have no built-in fallback address, so that an address it was not given is one it does not have.
    13. As a developer of the carry, I want the rule of which references are assets in a file of its own, testable by plain calls, so that I can change it without starting a server.
    14. As a developer of the carry, I want the digest list's format (written, read back, and a file's digest) in a file of its own, so that the term in the glossary has one home in the code (#26).
    15. As a developer of the carry, I want the log wording (bounded lists, shortened strings, one line) in a file of its own, so that every file that prints a stranger's string prints it the same way.
    16. As a developer of the carry, I want the flow in a file of its own that receives the live site's reader, so that its rules are tested against an in-memory live site.
    17. As a developer of the carry, I want the HTTP reader to hold only HTTP (redirect as an error, identity encoding, the byte ceilings, the timeout), so that the only cases that need a server are the ones about HTTP.
    18. As a developer of the carry, I want the status and content-type rules (a 404 counts against the 20, only CSS and JavaScript are carried, a missing list means could not look) to stay in the flow, so that they are tested without a server.
    19. As a developer, I want each file named for what it holds, so that I find a rule without opening the others.
    20. As a developer, I want each ceiling to live in the file that enforces it, so that a limit and its check are read together.
    21. As a developer, I want each comment to state today's reason beside its rule, with no history, so that the files read as what is true now.
    22. As a developer, I want the long header split so that each of its sections sits in the file whose rule it explains, so that no file explains another's rule.
    23. As a reviewer, I want every one of the 59 existing cases kept with its assertion, so that the split provably changes no behaviour.
    24. As a reviewer, I want the tests in one file per private file, so that a failing case points at the file that broke.
    25. As a reviewer, I want the slice-named test file gone once its cases have moved, so that no case runs twice or in two places.
    26. As a reviewer, I want the "string chunk counts as bytes" case to keep reaching the body reader directly, so that the one case the HTTP adapter cannot reach keeps its seam.
    27. As the maintainer, I want a row in the threat model for a settings writer moving the site's address, ending in its checks, so that the threat is no longer a blank.
    28. As the maintainer, I want the glossary to name the site's address, so that docs and specs use one word for it.
    29. As the maintainer, I want the workflow's own comments to say the address is reviewed code, so that the next reader does not reintroduce the variable as a convenience.
    30. As the maintainer, I want real use to be the merge's deploy and the deploy after I delete the variable, read the same, so that I know nothing reads the variable any more.
    31. As a future agent working on the carry, I want the structure to follow the levels, so that the structure check (#37) reads no mixed file here.

    Implementation Decisions

    • The site's address: the deploy workflow sets SITE_URL to the literal https://permitrulebook.com/ in its own environment. It no longer reads vars.SITE_URL, and the github.io fallback goes, because the literal is the address. The build, the sitemap, IndexNow and the carry keep reading SITE_URL as today; only its source changes. The workflow's header comment about the variable becomes: the address is this line, reviewed like any code, and no repository variable is read.
    • The variable is retired: after the merge, the human deletes the SITE_URL repository variable. Nothing reads it from the merge on.
    • The carry's entry stays where it is, and the deploy's command and its pinned block do not change. The entry reads CARRY_ORIGIN and the argv flags (--dist, --dry-run), passes the real HTTP reader to the flow, holds the two last-resort handlers, and exits 0 on every path. Its "Where the address comes from" comment becomes: from CARRY_ORIGIN, which the deploy sets from the reviewed address line, and from nowhere else. There is still no built-in fallback.
    • The deep module: a folder beside the entry holds five private files, one level each, each named for what it holds:
    • asset-names (rule): which references in a live page are this deploy's assets (readAssetNames, the name pattern and the name ceiling);
    • digest-list (rule, #26): the digest list's format, written and read back, its fixed header line, its line pattern and a file's digest;
    • log-wording (rule): what a stranger's string looks like in our log (boundedList, short, oneLine);
    • carry (flow): carryAssets, which reads what is live, compares it with this build, copies what is missing into dist/, writes the digest list, says which outcome happened and never fails;
    • live-site-over-http (network): liveAnswerOverHttp, and bodyWithin, which stays exported.
    • The seam: one function. The flow receives the live site as an input, not reaching for the network:
      liveAnswerFor(url, { byteCeiling, timeoutMs })
    → { ok: true, status, contentType, bytes }
    | { ok: false, why }

  carryAssets({ origin, dist, liveAnswerFor, dryRun, budgetMs })

    The HTTP adapter holds only HTTP: a redirect is an error, the body must be identity-encoded, a declared length over the ceiling is refused, bytes are counted as bytes, a body must match its declared length, and the timeout holds. Every rule about statuses and content types stays in the flow: a 404 counts against the 20, only CSS and JavaScript types are carried, and a missing or malformed digest list means could not look. The flow builds every URL from the origin, as today. why keeps its name, as the surrounding code spells it.

    • `dist/` is not behind a seam: the flow writes to the real folder it is given, since one real adapter would make a seam of ceremony only.
    • Ceilings move to the file that enforces them: the name ceiling to asset-names, the byte ceilings and the timeout to the flow's calls on the reader, and the count of 20 and the run budget to the flow.
    • Comments: each moves with its rule and states today's reason. Dates, review rounds, slice numbers and "it used to" narration go (CODING_STANDARDS.md, Rules: "No history"). The four sections of the header ("Where the address comes from", "What a page may cost this build", "What makes a carried byte trustworthy, and what does not", "What shapes this reads, and what it does not") each go to the file whose rule they explain. Comments elsewhere that point at the carrier's header (the workflow's step comment, the pipeline tests) point at the file that now holds the reason.
    • Threat model: a new row. Threat: a repository-settings writer points the deploy at a host they control (the carry publishes their bytes from our origin; canonical links and the sitemap name their host). Who: a writer of this repository's variables. Holds by, Checked: the site's address is a literal in the deploy workflow, reviewed like any code; the deploy reads no repository variable (the pipeline tests); the carry fetches only from the origin it is given and checks every carried byte against the previous build's digest list (the carry's tests).
    • Glossary: "The site's address" is defined under The deploy (written in the grill; committed with the slice's docs).
    • Bump: Z, bug, in v1.2.1.

    Testing Decisions

    • A good test here asserts a decision through a seam: what the carry carries, refuses, counts and says, or what the workflow reads. It never asserts how a function is built inside.
    • Seam 1, the flow: carryAssets with an in-memory liveAnswerFor over a map of URLs, on a throwaway temp dist/ per case, removed. Most of the existing cases land here: what is carried, what is refused and why, the count of 20 with 404s included, the digest check, the three outcomes and their last lines, and never failing.
    • Seam 2, the rules as plain calls: readAssetNames, the digest list's write and read, and the log wording.
    • Seam 3, the HTTP adapter on a real local server: liveAnswerOverHttp for a redirect as an error, encoding, declared and actual length, the ceilings and the timeout; bodyWithin directly for chunk reassembly and for a string chunk counted as bytes, the one case only the export reaches.
    • Seam 4, the deploy's text: the pipeline tests read the deploy workflow, as they already do for the carry step's pinned block and position. One new case asserts the workflow reads no repository variable (the vars context, named as the declared set). It is new behaviour, so it fails before the workflow changes. It does not assert the address's value.
    • Moved, not deleted: every one of the 59 existing cases keeps its assertion at the seam that owns it. A case's name changes only where it names the old file. The tests sit in one folder, one test file per private file (asset-names, digest-list, log-wording, carry, live-site-over-http), and the slice-named test file is deleted once it is empty.
    • Prior art: the existing carry suite (its temp dist/ per case and its local server helpers); the pipeline tests' reading of the workflow; the data repository's trust-line table tests, as the model of rule cases by plain call.
    • Green: the site's npm run build, npm test and npm run assets:check, on the branch.

    Out of Scope

    • The data repository's `vars.SITE_REPO`: the watch's rebuild signal names its target repository from a variable. A writer there can point the signal elsewhere, but the dispatch token is scoped to this site's repository alone, so the signal fails. The worst outcome is a missed rebuild, which the 06:40 schedule covers, and nobody's bytes are published. No issue.
    • Integrity hashes on our own assets (SRI): a separate defence. With the address fixed in reviewed code, the carry has no stranger's origin to defend against. No issue.
    • Any change to what the carry carries, refuses or says: this slice moves the carry and fixes its address. Its behaviour stays as it is.

    Further Notes

    • Real use comes right after the merge, since a merge to master deploys:
    • The merge's deploy: it builds at https://permitrulebook.com/. Its carry step's last lines give one of the three outcomes, as on the deploy before it. asset-digests.txt and the sitemap are live and name the same host.
    • The human deletes the SITE_URL repository variable.
    • The next deploy (the 06:40 schedule or a dispatch) reads the same: same base, same sitemap host, same carry outcome.

    The session reads both deploys and posts the reading. The human's steps are the deletion and the word.

    • Decisions: the grill's 13 decisions are recorded on #46.

    Tickets

    • #111 The site's address is reviewed code, and the deploy reads no repository variable
    • #112 The carry's rules stand on their own: asset names, the digest list, the log wording
    • #113 The carry's flow is given the live site, and only HTTP needs a server
